<!-- page 482 -->
Board of Supervisors Regular 3. q.        
Meeting Date: 01/12/2021  
Title: Support for Improvement and Expansion of Wht Mtn Trail System TRACKS
Submitted By: Melissa Buckley, Clerk of the Board of Supervisors
Department: Board of Supervisors
Motion before the Board:
Resolution Number 3-21 supporting the improvement and expansion of the White Mountain
Trail System South of Show Low, Arizona
Background:
TRACKS is a volunteer organization formed in 1987 dedicated to using, promoting, preserving,
and protecting multi-use trails throughout the White Mountains. TRACKS has partnered with
another new local organization, Save the Buena Vista Foundation (SBVF) which has interest in
improving existing and developing new trails in the Buena Vista Trail are and Chihuahua Pines
Connector corridor south of the City of Show Low. Both organizations have been working with
the USFS to create a trail improvement plan for these areas. Jointly these organizations are asking
the USFS to officially designate approximately eleven miles of existing "social trails" within the
Buena Vista area into the official WMTS trail system and for permission to build approximately
eighteen miles of new trail.

RESOLUTION NO. __________ 
 
A RESOLUTION OF THE BOARD OF SUPERVISORS OF NAVAJO COUNTY, 
ARIZONA, SUPPORTING THE IMPROVEMENT AND EXPANSION OF THE WHITE 
MOUNTAIN TRAIL SYSTEM SOUTH OF SHOW LOW, ARIZONA. 
 
 
 
WHEREAS, the Navajo County Board of Supervisors is aware of a trail development 
plan proposed by TRACKS and SAVE THE BUENA VISTA FOUNDATION (SBVF) to the 
United States Forest Service which would substantially improve and expand the existing White 
Mountain Trail System (WMTS) south of Show Low, Arizona; and 
 
WHEREAS, the proposed trail development plan would officially designate 
approximately eleven miles of existing “social trails” within the Buena Vista Trail area, and call 
for the creation of approximately eighteen new miles of new trail within the Buena Vista Trail 
area and within the existing Chihuahua Pines Trail Corridor which links the Buena Vista Trail to 
the Los Caballos Trail; and  
 
WHEREAS, high-quality non-motorized trails have a positive impact on economic 
development through the creation of tourism, directly benefitting the restaurant, lodging sectors, 
as well as other sectors of the economy; and  
 
WHEREAS, high-quality non-motorized trail systems can drive economic development 
by making Navajo County more attractive to individuals, families and business; and  
 
WHEREAS, the development of the trail system as proposed would improve 
opportunities for recreation for local residents and visitors to Navajo County;  
 
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED by the Board as follows: 
 
1.)  The Board of Supervisors finds that the proposed trail expansion and improvement 
proposal submitted to the USFS by TRACKS and SAVE THE BUENA VISTA 
FOUNDATION, attached hereto as “Exhibit A,” will promote economic development 
and recreational opportunities by substantially improving the existing WMTS south 
of Show Low, Arizona.  
 
2.) The Board of Supervisors hereby officially supports the trail development master plan 
proposed by TRACKS and SAVE THE BUENA VISTA FOUNDATION, and urges 
the United States Forest Service to consider and implement the plan to the greatest 
extent that is practical and feasible.

Board of Supervisors Regular 5. a.        
Meeting Date: 01/12/2021  
Title: NAUA 9-1-1 Presentation
Submitted For: David Clouse, Sheriff 
Submitted By: Melissa Buckley, Clerk of the Board of Supervisors
Department: Sheriff 
Presented By: Sheriff David Clouse
Subject:
Presentation on the New Text to 911 Capability
Background: 
The Northeastern Arizona 9-1-1 Users Association (NAUA), which is comprised of the
Navajo County Sheriff's Office, Apache County Sheriff's Office, Show Low Regional
Communications Center & the White Mountain Apache Tribal Police Department, all now
have the ability to accept emergency text messages. 
 
Residents and visitors can now text 9-1-1 instead of calling if they are unable to do so in
the case of an emergency. This new technology is available to use from their cell phones
when speaking on the phone is not an option or could put the caller in danger.
Text-to-9-1-1 is an added resource for individuals who are deaf, hard of hearing, or have
speech disabilities.

▪ Text-to-9-1-1 will offer many benefits including 
enhanced access for people with disabilities, alternative 
means of emergency communications and availability 
and ease of use for the general public. 
▪ Text to 9-1-1 is intended for use by those who are in a 
medical situation and unable to speak; for those who 
need to summon emergency services quietly in 
dangerous situations (IE: domestic violence scenarios, 
hostages, home burglaries in progress, etc); for those 
whose call will simply not go through due to poor 
reception and for those who are deaf, hard of hearing or 
speech disabled.

and follow

Navajo County - For Official Use Only
▪ Text location information is not equal to current location technology.
▪ As with all text messages, 9-1-1 messages can take longer to receive, 
can get out of order or may not be received.
▪ Text-to-9-1-1 is not available if you are roaming.
▪ A text or data plan is required to place a text-to-9-1-1
▪ If texting to 9-1-1 is not available in your area, or is temporarily 
unavailable, you will receive a message indicating that texting 9-1-1 is 
not available and to contact 9-1-1 by other means.
▪ Photos and videos cannot be sent to 9-1-1 at this time.
▪ Text-to-9-1-1 cannot include more than one person. Do not send your 
emergency text to anyone other than 9-1-1.
▪ Always remember, CALL IF YOU CAN AND TEXT IF YOU CAN’T! 
▪ Please don’t text and drive!

Background:
For FFY 2021, Arizona State WIC worked on a project to offer breastfeeding peer counseling to
all Arizona agencies. Mountain Park Health Center has been assigned and awarded the
Breastfeeding Peer Counseling Special Project to offer services to agencies who do not have the
Breastfeeding Peer Counseling programs. Mountain Park will be supporting all WIC
participants within Navajo County who have an interest in breastfeeding support.

<!-- page 504 -->
MEMORANDUM OF UNDERSTANDING 
Between 
MOUNTAIN PARK HEALTH CENTER 
and 
NAVAJO COUNTY PUBLIC HEALTH SERVICES DISTRICT - NAVAJO COUNTY 
WIC PROGRAM 
 
This Memorandum of Understanding (“MOU”) is entered into between Mountain Park Health 
Center WIC Program (hereafter “MPHC”) and Navajo County Public Health Services District - 
Navajo County WIC Program (hereafter the “Partnering W IC Agency”) and together referred to 
as the “Parties”.  
 
Background 
 
1. The Arizona Department of Health Services (ADHS) Bureau of Nutrition and Physical 
Activity (BNPA) administers funds provided by the United States Department of Agriculture 
(USDA) for the operation of the Special Supplemental Nutrition Program for Women, 
Infants and Children (WIC) and Breastfeeding Peer Counseling (BFPC) Program  for the 
State of Arizona.  In 2003, the USDA Food and Nutrition Service (FNS) entered into a 
cooperative agreement with Best Start Social Marketing (Best Start) to gather information to 
obtain a clear understanding of the components that are necessary to sustain effective peer 
counseling programs and how to structure these programs so they are cost effective and 
manageable. Subsequently, a program was developed based upon recommendations from the 
USDA for a training and technical assistance project to be used as a model to aid WIC in 
designing, building, maintaining, and sustaining peer counseling programs that will improve 
breastfeeding initiation and duration rates. Arizona uses, and will continue to use, a USDA 
recommended program to guide and develop the state peer counseling program. 
2. MPHC operates The Special Supplemental Nutrition Program for WIC (the “MPHC WIC 
Program”), and a Breastfeeding Peer Counseling (BFPC) Program on behalf of ADHS.   
Combining peer counseling with the ongoing breastfeeding promotion efforts in WIC has the 
potential to significantly impact breastfeeding rates among WIC participants, and, most 
significantly, increase the harder-to-achieve breastfeeding duration rates. The discretionary 
peer counseling services are considered an enhanced support to WIC breastfeeding services 
to help achieve the WIC Nutrition Services objectives regarding breastfeeding.  
3. As part of its agreement with ADHS, MPHC has also been assigned and awarded a 
Breastfeeding Pe er Counseling Special Project (BFPC Special Project) to provide BFPC 
services to various agencies, such as Partnering WIC Agency.

<!-- page 505 -->
4. In the performance of its agreement with ADHS, MPHC employs qualified staff to operate 
the MPHC BFPC Program including Breastfeeding Peer Counselors, a Breastfeeding Peer 
Counselor Supervisor, and a WIC Director. 
Purpose 
 
1. This non-financial MOU sets forth the terms and agreement between the MPHC and 
Partnering WIC Agency, to clearly identify the roles a nd responsibilities of each party as 
they relate to the BFPC Special Project services requirement set forth by ADHS. 
 
2. MPHC and Partnering WIC Agency shall begin a partnership, t he goal of which is to 
improve breastfeeding initiation and duration rates amongst BFPC participants. 
 
I. MPHC’s Responsibilities 
1. MPHC shall c oordinate with Partnering WIC Agency to assist Partnering WIC Agency in 
providing breastfeeding peer counseling services  within the Par tnering WIC Agency 
location.  
2. MPHC shall assist Partnering WIC Agency by:  
a. Accepting referrals generated from the Partnering WIC Agency; and  
b. Performing all the work requ ired to administer and provide additional 
breastfeeding support services to eligible BFPC participants referred by 
Partnering WIC Agency; and  
c. Following the USDA recommended best practice guidelines for BFPC services in 
order to increase the breastfeeding initiation and duration rates. 
3. MPHC shall employ and provide at least one (1)  Breastfeeding Peer Counselor, who will 
report to the MPHC Breastfeeding Peer Counselor Supervisor and/or WIC Director.   
4. MPHC will be responsible for adhering to the terms and conditions required of it to 
perform pursuant to its agreement with ADHS relating to any WIC services, as well as all 
applicable BFPC policies as outlined in Chapter 19 of the Arizona WIC P olicy and 
Procedure Manual (“Chapter 19 of the WIC Manual”). 
5. MPHC Breastfeeding Peer Counselor(s) shall perform services only within their scope of 
practice.  Th ese BFPC Counselors will be trained to identify situations that are outside of 
their sco pe of practice , which will allow them to appropriately and accordingly refer 
participants to designated WIC personnel in a t imely manner as needed (see Appendix E 
of Chapter 19 of the WIC Manual: Peer Counselor Program – When to Yield). 
a. MPHC Peer Counselor Job Parameters include, b ut are not limited to: 
i. Availability to support participants outside usual clinic hours;

<!-- page 506 -->
ii. The peer counselor shall follow the general c ontact protocol for each 
participating woman on at least a monthly contact basis; and 
iii. Virtual prenatal/breastfeeding classes/support gro ups. 
6. MPHC shall conduct meetings with Partnering WIC Agency on at least a quarterly basis 
to discuss status and progress, and work toward continuous quality improvement of the 
BFPC Program. 
7. MPHC shall s ubmit quarterly reports to ADHS for the BFPC Peer Counseling Program 
within 15 days after each quarter of every year MPHC is contracted with ADHS.  
II. Partnering WIC Agency’s Responsibilities  
1. Partnering WIC Agency shall c oordinate with MPHC to provide breastfeeding peer 
counseling services as desc ribed in this MOU at Partnering WIC Agency. 
2. Partnering WIC A gency will be responsible for adhering to the terms and  conditions 
required of it to perfor m pursuant to its agreement with AD HS relating to any BFPC 
services, as well as all applicable BFPC policies as outlined in Chapter 19 of the WIC 
Manual. 
3. Solely for p urposes of performing services under this MOU, Partnering WIC Agency 
expressly authorizes ADHS to allow MPHC to securely access Partnering WIC Agency’s 
Health and Nutrition Delivery System (HANDS) data. 
a. Partnering WIC Agency shall coordinate access of appro priate HANDS data with 
designated MPHC staf f and  provide appropriate HANDS login access , if 
necessary; 
b. Partnering WIC A gency shall provide any additional authorization to ADHS  
regarding MPHC’s authorization to HANDS data as may be required. 
4. Partnering WIC Agency shall promote and offer BFPC Program services to all eligible 
WIC participants.  
a. At a minimum, services are to be offered to eligible WIC participants, at each 
Certification; and   
b. If the WIC participant agrees to participate in the BFPC Program, Partnering WIC 
Agency shall: (i) complete the “Referral to Peer Counselor” form, (ii) scan it into 
the HANDS system, (iii) document in the Tool, Goal(s), Information and Follow-
up (TGIF) note, and (iv) send a secure email to the designated MPHC staff 
notifying MPHC of the referral. 
5. Partnering WIC Agency shall determine a point liaison who will receive referrals from 
the peer counselor when presented with a situation outside of their scope of practice, 
including but not limited to, developing a support referral s ystem for peer counselors with

<!-- page 507 -->
community Lactation Consultants, including International Board-Certified Lactation 
Consultants (IBCLCs). 
6. Partnering WIC Agency will collaborate with ADHS to provide the USDA recommended 
Peer Counseling training to its WIC staff. 
7. Partnering WI C Agency shall a ttend quarterly meetings with MPHC , and other 
participating agencies, to c ommunicate, collaborate and work toward  continuous quality 
improvement of the BFPC Program. 
a. Partnering WIC Agency shall provide MPHC with, and or s ubmit to ADHS, any 
information and/or data necessary for MPHC to submit required reports to ADHS, 
as required per the BFPC Program. 
b. Partnering WIC A gency shall collaborate with the MPHC liaison to report any 
concerns, and to regularly communicate progress o f the services being provided. 
III.  Joint Representations 
The parties represent and warrant the following: 
1. All clinicians employed or co ntracted by MPHC and Partnering WIC Agency  who 
furnish services to individuals under this MOU will: (i) be d uly licensed, certified, and/or 
otherwise qualified to provide services in the State of Arizona; and (ii) furnish services in 
accordance with all re levant federal, state, and local laws and regulations, includ ing, but 
not limited to, non-discrimination laws. 
2. Each part y and its cli nicians providing services hereunder are cover ed by a professional 
liability insurance policy or a self -insurance program (malpractice, errors, and omissions) 
that provides sufficient coverage against professional liabilities that may arise fr om acts 
or om issions in connection with or related to the services furnished pursuant to this 
MOU. 
3. Each party i s solely and  exclusively liable for services it furnishes to individuals in 
connection with any WIC program, in cluding but not limite d to the BFPC  Program; 
MPHC shall not assume any liability associated with Partnering WIC Agency’s provision 
of any services provided to  any individual, a nd Partnering WIC Agency shall not assume 
any liability associated with MPHC’s provision of any services by MP HC to any 
individual. 
4. All health an d health -related professionals employed by or under contract with either 
party retain sole and complete d iscretion, subject to any v alid restriction(s) imposed by 
participation in a managed care plan, to refer pa rticipants to any and all providers who 
best meet the medical needs of such patients. 
5. All parties acknowledge that all participants have the freedom to choose (and/or req uest 
referral to) any provider of servic es, and MPHC and Partnering WIC Agency will advise 
participants of such right, subject to any val id restriction(s) imposed by participation in a 
managed care plan.

<!-- page 508 -->
6. This MOU is neither a fiscal n or a funds obligation document nor is either party granting 
rights to the other with respect to intellectual  property. The Parties acknowledge and 
agree that t hey have freely negotiated the ter ms of this MOU and that neither Party has 
offered or received a ny inducement or othe r consideration in exchange for entering into 
this MOU.  Nothing in this MOU requires, is intended to require, or provides payment or 
benefit of any kind (directly or indir ectly), for the referral of individuals or business to 
either P arty by the other Par ty.  The Part ies further acknowledge that  no monetary 
compensation shall be exchanged u nder this MOU and there shall be no charge for the 
use of the space or equipment, or participation by either Party’s personnel pursuant to this 
MOU.  Except as provided herein, the p rovisions of this Section sh all not prohibit 
Collaborator from obtaining c ompensation for care provided at any of Collaborato r’s 
clinics, consistent with its normal business practices. 
a. Should there be any occurrence, endea vor or transfer of an ything of val ue 
involving reimbursement or contribution of funds between the parties to  this 
MOU, at any time, it will be handled in accor dance with applicable laws, 
regulations and procedures. Such occurrences will be outlined in sepa rate 
agreements that shall be made  in writing by representativ es of the Parties and 
shall be independently a uthorized by appropriate statutory authority.  Each  party 
shall be fiscally responsible for their own portion of work performed under this 
MOU. 
7. The parties retain the au thority to co ntract with other providers.  This MOU in no way 
restricts either party fr om participating in similar activities with other p ublic or private 
agencies, organizations and individuals. 
IV. Use of Information 
The Parties will com ply with all laws and  regulations governing the privacy and se curity of 
health information, including withou t limitation the Health Insurance Por tability and 
Accountability Act, as amended and applicable regulations promulgated thereunder ("HIPAA'').  
Both parties will treat a s confidential all information it may rec eive regarding diagnosis, history 
or treatment that allows unique identification of  an individual ('"Protected Health Information"), 
in accordance with 45 CFR § 164.  To the extent required by a pplicable law, the Pa rties will 
implement and maintain such pr ivacy and security safeguards as are necessary  to ensure that 
Protected Health Info rmation is ade quately protected from unauthorized access, and that any 
disclosure of such information is compli ant with applicable H IPAA requirements.  Both parties 
shall ensure that all services provided hereunder are performed in accordance with appropriate 
patient consents, where applicable, and all applicable laws, including without limitation, HIPAA. 
V. Indemnification 
Each Party sha ll indemnify and hold the other Party har mless from any and all claims, causes of 
action, and demands whatsoever made for loss, damage, or  injury to any person arising from a 
breach by such Party, or its subcontractors, of its obligations under this MOU or  state law. 
 
VI. Regulatory Restrictions

<!-- page 509 -->
Each Party shall warrant that it is not currently listed by a state or federal agency as excluded or  
otherwise ineligible for participation in any state or federal health care program.   Each Party  
agrees that it will not employ, c ontract with, or otherwise u se the services of any individual 
whom it knows or should have known, after reasonable inquiry: 
• has been convicted of a criminal offense related to health care (unless the individual has 
been reinstated to participation in Medicaid, Medicare and all other state or federal health 
care programs after being excluded because of the conviction), or  
• is cur rently listed by a state or federal agency as excluded, debarred, or otherwise 
ineligible for participation in any sta te or federal  health care program and fur ther agrees 
that it will immediately notify the other in the event that it, or any person in its employ, 
has been excluded, debarred, or has otherwise become ineligible for participation in any 
state or federal heal th care progr am.  Each Party agrees to co ntinue to make reasonable 
inquiry regarding the status of its employees and independent contracto rs on a regular 
basis. 
 
VII. Retention and Inspection of Records 
 
The parties shall retain all data and other records ("rec ords") relating to this MOU for a period of 
three (3) years after the completion of eac h annual contract year. All records shall be subjec t to 
inspection and audit by ADHS at reasonable times. The parties shall provide ADHS the right of 
access to its facil ities, server s, at all reasonable times, in order to monitor and evaluate 
performance, compliance, and/or quality assurance under this MOU. 
 
VIII. Non-Discrimination 
The Parties shall comply with Executive Order 75 -5 as modified by Executive  Order 2009 -09, 
which mandates that all persons, regardless of  race, color, religion, sex, age, national ori gin or 
political affiliation, shall have equal acce ss to employment opportunities, and all other applicable 
State and Federal employment laws, rules , and regulations, in cluding the A mericans with 
Disabilities Act. The Parties shall take affirmative action to ensure that applicants for 
employment and employ ees are not discriminated against due to race, creed, color, religion, sex, 
national origin or disability. 
 
IX. Term and Termination 
The term of this MOU shall commence on October 1, 2020  and continue through September 30, 
2021, (“Initial Term”) unless terminated at an earlier date in accordance with this Section .  This 
MOU will automatically renew for four (4) additional one (1) year ter ms unless written notice of 
intent not to renew is provided by one party t o the other party no less than thirty (30) days prior 
to the expiration of the then current MOU. 
 
1. This MOU may be terminated, in whole or in part, a t any time upon the m utual 
agreement of MPHC and Partnering WIC Agency. 
2. Either MPHC or Partnering WIC Agency may terminate this MOU without cause upon 
thirty (30) days prior written notice to the other party.

<!-- page 510 -->
3. This MOU may be terminated for cause upon wri tten notice by either MPHC or 
Partnering WIC Agency .  "Caus e" shall include, but is not limited to : (i) a ma terial 
breach of any term of this MOU, subject to a thirty (30) day opportun ity to cure; (ii) any 
material change in the financial condition of either party that reasonably indicates that 
such p arty will be unable to fulfill its obligations pursuant to this MOU; (iii) the loss of 
required insurance by either party; (iv) either party’s loss, revoc ation, or suspension of 
any license or other authorizati on to do business; (v) exclusion or suspension  of either 
party from participation in federal and/or state health care programs; and (vi) the good 
faith determination by MPHC that the h ealth, welfare, and/or safety of patients  receiving 
care from Partnering WIC Agency is jeopardized by the continuation of this MOU. 
 
X. Amendment or Modificati ons 
No amendment or modifications to this MOU, including any amendment or modification of this 
paragraph, shall be effective unless the same is in writing signed by the Parties. 
XI. Arizona Law 
The law of Arizona applies to this MOU including, where applicable, the Uniform Commercial 
Code as adopted by the State of Arizona. 
XII. Relationship of Parties 
The parties under this MOU are independent contractors. Neither party to this MOU shall be 
deemed to be the employee or agent of the other party to the MOU. 
XIII. Severability 
The Provisions of this MOU are severable. Any term or condition deemed illegal or invalid shall 
not affect any other term or condition of the MOU.  
XIV. Dispute Resolution 
In the event of any dispute, claim, question, or disagreement ar ising from or relating to this 
MOU, the parties shall consult and negotiate with each other in good faith and, recognizing their 
mutual interests, attempt to reach a just and equitable solution satisfactory to both parties. If no 
resolution can be reached after thirty (30) days, either party may immediately terminate this 
MOU. 
XV. Entire Agreement 
This MOU constitutes the entire agreement between the parties, and supersedes all prior 
agreements, proposals, negotiations, representations or communications relating to the subject 
matter.  This MOU may be modified only by a written instrument executed by both parties.

<!-- page 511 -->
XVI. Headings 
Headings are for organizational purposes only and shall not be interpreted as having legal 
significance or meaning. 
XVII. Notices 
 
All notices must be in writing and effected by personal delivery, or by an overnight courier 
which provides receipts, or regular first-class mail, postage prepaid and properly addressed to the 
Parties at the following addresses or such other addresses as the Parties may d esignate in writing

Title: United States Geological Survey Stream Monitoring Joint Funding Agreement 
Submitted For: John Osgood, Public Works Director 
Submitted By: John Osgood, Public Works Director
Department: Public Works
Motion before the Board:
Consider approval of a Joint Funding Agreement with the United States Geological Survey
(USGS)for the period of October 1, 2020, to September 30, 2023, for operating and maintaining
seven (7) surface water gaging stations
Background:
Navajo County Department of Public Works has teamed with the U.S. Geological Survey
(USGS) over many years to operate and maintain surface water gaging stations.  We have
attached the state map showing where our seven (7) gages are located.  This partnership is
important in having data collected and stored by another responsible entity that documents
the flows in the main rivers and contributing tributaries in the County, and contributes to
important mutually beneficial water resources studies.
 
Recommendation: Staff recommends executing the contract with USGS.

B. Streamflow-gaging – Elevated flow only
At the Little Colorado River at Holbrook and Puerco River near Chambers sites the sand channel 
moves regularly, and the sensors often becomes isolated from the flow or buried. This results in a 
loss of data quality for low flows. In order to address that issue and to ensure that the data 
provided to Navajo County meets their needs for monitoring and reporting the timing and 
magnitude of high flow events operations have been modified. 
For the Puerco River near Chambers operations will focus on monitoring, measuring and 
publishing flows when the instantaneous values (15 minute data) are greater than 500ft3/s. A 
banner statement is on the USGS website informing viewers of this focus and a threshold line at 
500ft3/s can be seen on the discharge hydrograph screening out any data below that value. 
For the Little Colorado River at Holbrook operations will focus on monitoring, measuring and 
publishing flows when the instantaneous values (15 minute data) are greater than 50ft3/s. A 
banner statement is on the USGS website informing viewers of this focus and a threshold line at 
50ft3/s can be seen on the discharge hydrograph screening out any data below that value. 
Because the focus is now on higher flows the cost of operations at these sites has been reduced.

Board of Supervisors Regular 8. b. 1.        
Meeting Date: 01/12/2021  
Title: Zoning Ordinance Text Amendment- Distance between buildings on the same lot
Submitted For: John Osgood, Public Works Director 
Submitted By: Berrin Nejad, Community Development Director
Department: Public Works Division: Planning & Zoning
Presented By: Berrin Nejad
Motion before the Board:
PUBLIC HEARING: Consideration and possible adoption of Resolution Number 4-21
approving/denying text amendments to the Zoning Ordinance modifying the Intensity of Use Regulations
Section of Articles 3,4,5,6,8,9,11,13, and 14 regarding the distance between buildings on the same lot.
Background:
The current Navajo County Zoning Ordinance indicates the distance between buildings must be a
minimum of fifteen (15) feet on the same lot in the section of “Intensity of Use Regulations” in each of the
residential zoning districts.  This regulation originally comes from the Fire Code and the International
Residential Code (IRC). In the 2015 IRC which was adopted by the BOS approximately two years ago, the
regulation allows a minimum distance of five (5) feet.  Staff has discussed internally and with several Fire
Departments on the subject and all support and agree the five-foot requirement is acceptable.  
 
With all incoming building permits being reviewed by different departments and the current Zoning
Ordinance still requiring fifteen feet, conflicts are occurring in approving the permits.  With this
amendment, this requirement will be five feet in all codes.
 
The Planning and Zoning Commission heard this item at their December 17, 2020,
meeting and recommended approval by a vote of 4-0. 
The articles involved from the Zoning Ordinance in this amendment: 
Article 3 A-General General Zoning District Section 306
Article 4 Rural -20 Rural Zoning District (20 acre/du) Section 406
Article 5 Rural-10 Rural Zoning District (10 acre/du) Section 506
Article 6 Rural-5 Rural Zoning District (5 acre/du) Section 606
Article 8 Rural-1 Rural Zoning District (1 acre/du) Section 806
Article 9 R1-43 Single Family Residential Section 906
Article 11 R1-10 Single Family Residential Section 1106
Article 13 R-2 Multiple-Family Residential Section 1306
Article 14 R-3 Multiple-Family Residential Section 1406

<!-- page 536 -->
Articles excluded from this amendment: 
Commercial and Industrial zones excluded since there are no setback regulations between buildings in these
zones.
  
Article 15 C-R Commercial-Residential N/A
Article 18 IND-1 Light Industrial N/A
Article 19 IND-2 Heavy Industrial N/A

EXHIBIT 1 
 
 
 
From: Brian Russell <BRussell@timbermesafire.org>  
Sent: Wednesday, December 9, 2020 12:55 PM 
To: Berrin Nejad <berrin.nejad@navajocountyaz.gov> 
Subject: RE: 5-foot separation between the buildings 
 
 
 
Berrin, 
 
From the standpoint of Timber Mesa, if a structure is built within the requirements of the building and 
fire codes as adopted, there is no issue with allowing 5’ spacing between structures on the same lot as

ORDINANCE 
 WHEREAS, Planning & Zoning Staff has recommended the amendment of the 
Navajo County Zoning Ordinance, modifying Intensity of Use Regulations  Section of 
Articles 3,4,5,6,8,9,11,13, and 14  regarding the distance between buildings on the same 
lot; and

Planning & Zoning Commission on December 17, 2020; and

testimony and other evidence presented at the hearing, as well as the recommendations of 
staff, found that the zoning ordinance amendments are co nsistent with the public health, 
safety, and general welfare and should be approved as set forth herein; and

of Supervisors  that Articles 3,4,5,6,8,9,11,13, and 14  are amended within the Navajo  
County Zoning Ordinance as follows:

Board of Supervisors Regular 8. b. 2.        
Meeting Date: 01/12/2021  
Title: AZ3 Kaywood Wireless Communication Facility - District V - SUP
Submitted For: Berrin Nejad, Community Development Director 
Submitted By: Cody Cooper, Planner I
Department: Public Works Division: Planning & Zoning
Presented By: Cody Cooper
Motion before the Board:
PUBLIC HEARING: Consideration and possible adoption of Resolution Number 5-21
approving/denying a Special Use Permit to construct and operate a 105' wireless communication
facility on APN 212-20-147D, a portion of Township 9 North, Range 22 East, Section 22 of the
Gila and Salt River Meridian.
Background:
The proposed project is a 105' monopole tower, to be situated within a 30' x 30' lease area.  The
monopole tower is proposed to be of monopine design and have the ground equipment enclosed
by a 7' wooden fence.
The subject property was previously granted a Special Use Permit for a wireless communication
facility, not to exceed 75 feet in height, by Board of Supervisors Resolution 18-13 on June 11,
2013.
The applicants have indicated that their need for this tower is driven by the fact that there are no
power poles, buildings, or existing cell sites (even with extension) to collocate upon, or that are
tall enough in the vicinity to meet their Radio Frequency expectations and needs.
This item was recommended for approval by the Navajo County Planning and Zoning
Commission by a vote of 3-1 at its meeting of November 19, 2020, subject to conditions
outlined in the Staff Report and Resolution for Approval. 
Recommendation: 
Staff and the Navajo County Planning and Zoning Commission recommend approval of the
Special Use Permit, subject to the conditions stated within the Staff Report.

NAVAJO N  
NAVAJO COUNTY PUBLIC WORKS DEPARTMENT 
PLANNING & ZONING 
 
 
STAFF REPORT  
BOARD OF SUPERVISORS 
 
Hearing Date: January 12, 2021 
Case No:  SUP 20-008 
Action:   Special Use Permit 
Applicant/Owner: Verizon Wireless 
Project Name: AZ23 Kaywood Wireless Communication Facility   
PARCEL INFORMATION  
 Address: 3044 Lockwood Drive 
 
 APN:  212-20-147D 
 
 Legal Desc: Township 9 North, Range 22 East, Section 22 of the Gila and Salt River  
  Meridian in the Lakeside area. 
 
 District: V 
 
 Area:  Lakeside, AZ 
 
 Parcel Size: Approximately 3.88 acres. 
 
STATED REASON FOR REQUEST:  
 
The applicant has requested a Special Use Permit to construct and operate a wireless communication 
facility. 
 
SURROUNDING USES:  
 
Direction Zoning/Use 
North R1-10, Church 
East R1-10, Single-Family Residential 
South R1-10, Single-Family Residential 
West R1-10, Single-Family Residential

<!-- page 548 -->
BACKGROUND: 
 
This property was previously granted a Special Use Permit for a wireless cellular tower via Board of 
Supervisors Resolution 18 -13 on June 11, 2013.  The approval of this Special Use Permit was based 
upon the following conditions: 
 
1. The Special Use Permit shall permit one wireless communication tower, not to exceed 75 feet in 
height, on the subject property. 
 
2. The permitted Special Use shall be allowed to occur only in the location shown on the approved site 
plan. 
 
3. The permitted Special Use shall run with the land. 
 
4. The applicant shall obtain a building permit for such tower. 
 
5. The applicant must meet all State and Federal requirements concerning communication towers prior 
to any building permits being issued. 
 
6. If the tower is no longer being utilized, the owner shall cause at his expense the removal of all 
components of this tower within a 90-day period. 
 
7. The climbing pegs shall be removed from the bottom 20 feet of the tower as a safety precaution to 
the public. 
 
8. The owner shall provide all reasonable cooperation to other public and private users who may be 
interested in co -location on this tower and shall consider such co -location upon commercially 
reasonable terms unless such co-location is not technically feasible. 
 
9. The owner shall paint the structure an earth tone color that will blend in with the surrounding area, 
as well as maintain the paint on the pole and antennae. 
 
The applicant held a public outreach meeting for this project on March 12 th, 2020 at 5:00 p.m.  
Notification of this hearing was sent 10 days prior to this meeting to all neighbors within 300’ of the 
project boundaries.  Sign-in sheets indicate that four (4) members of the public attended this meeting.  
The applicant has indicated that one attendee, and their spouse, indicated concerns over potential health 
issues with the proposed tower.  In response to this, the applicant offered to share resources with these 
members of the public to mitigate concerns.  The summary of this meeting indicates that the other three 
(3) attendees were in support of the new tower. 
 
Previously, three neighbors had voiced objection over the placement of a cellular tower on this property.  
With a monopine design, all three have stated they withdraw their objections to the tower, with two 
stating support for the tower if it has the monopine design. 
 
At a Planning and Zoning Commission Hearing on November 19, 2020, SUP 20-008 was recommended 
for approval by the Commission by a vote of 3-1.  This recommendation of approval was subject to the 
conditions outlined in this report. 
 
PROJECT DESCRIPTION:

<!-- page 549 -->
The applicant is proposing the construction of a 105’ monopole tower, to be situated within a 30’ x 30’ 
lease area.  They are proposing the monopole be of monopine design and having the ground equipment 
enclosed by an 8’ wooden fence.   
 
The applicant has indicated the need for this tower is driven by the fact that there are no power poles, 
buildings, or existing cell sites (even with extension) to collocate on, or that are tall eno ugh in the 
vicinity to meet their RF (Radio Frequency) expectations and needs. 
 
ZONING DISTRICTS: 
ARTICLE 11 – (R1-10) SINGLE-FAMILY RESIDENTIAL ZONING DISTRICT 10,000 
SQUARE FEET PER DWELLING UNIT 
 
Section 1101 – Purpose. 
 
The principal purpose of this zoning district is to conserve and protect single-family residential 
development where minimum lots of not less than ten-thousand (10,000) square feet in area are 
suitable and appropriate taking into consideration existing conditions, including presen t use of 
land, present lot sizes, future land use needs, and the availability of public utilities.  Principle 
uses permitted in this zoning district include single-family dwellings, churches, schools, parks, 
playgrounds, and other community facilities. 
 
ZONING ORDINANCE: 
Article 20 – Special Uses and Planned Unit Developments 
 
Section 2001 – Special Uses 
 
34. Television, wireless towers, and radio transmitter towers and stations, subject to 
approval by Federal Regulatory Agencies. 
 
COMPREHENSIVE PLAN/AREA PLAN:  
Comprehensive Plan 
 
4.0 Land Use Element 
 
4.7 Goals and Policies 
 
Goal 6: Maintain compatible land use patterns while encouraging free market development. 
 
Policy 6-B: Consider, if needed, providing for buffers between different land uses including, 
but not limited to, setbacks, walls or fences, and landscaping. 
 
- The applicant is meeting, and even exceeding, the extended setback standards for taller 
structures like cellular towers.  Additionally, they are looking to design their tower with a 
monopine design to blend with the natural environment as much as possible.   Beyond this, 
they will be surrounding their ground equipment with a solid, wooden fence to screen this 
equipment as best as possible.

<!-- page 550 -->
Goal 9: Actively plan to accommodate growth in Navajo County in an appropriate manner. 
 
Policy 9-F: Encourage development/installation of technology improvements including, but 
not limited to, high speed Internet lines and other communications improvements in Navajo 
County. 
 
- The applicant has indicated they intend to erect this tower as there are no other suitable sites 
nearby that can meet the needs to improve communications for the local area.  They believe 
that this location will provide better communications within this portion of the County. 
 
ANALYSIS: 
 
There are not many goals related to wireless communications facilities within the Navajo County 
Comprehensive Plan, but the applicant’s proposal has met the criteria that do exist.  The applicant has 
met higher levels of setbacks to keep the tower internal ly located on the property as a sort of buffer.  
They have also proposed alternative designing of their tower to blend in with the natural environment 
to the greatest extent possible.  Additionally, the Comprehensive Plan states that, to accommodate 
growth properly, development that improves communications in Navajo County should be encouraged. 
 
The applicant has stated they intend incorporate a monopine design to provide some form of blending 
with the natural environment. The request for an 8’ solid wood fence that surrounds the accompanying 
equipment for the tower exceeds the allowances for fence height as outlined by Navajo County’s 
adopted Building Code.  As a result, Staff has amended this request to reflect an allowable, 7’ solid 
wood fence to surround said equipment. 
 
Staff has had discussions with the applicant over a non-buildable easement over the potential fall zone 
for this wireless communications tower.  The applicant indicated within their submittal that the landlord 
and Verizon are asking this condition not to be applied as this was not applied to the previous tower on 
the parcel and the landlord feels renders the area useless.  A letter was provided on behalf of the 
applicant by Michael E. Gibbons, a Civil Engineer, regarding the fall zone.  Fo llowing review of this 
letter and of documents provided for the cellular tower, the Navajo County Engineering Department has 
requested that a non-buildable easement of 105 feet be provided around the base of the tower for public 
safety. 
 
If the conditions outlined in this report, as prepared by the Navajo County Planning and Zoning and 
Engineering Departments are applied to this project, Staff feels this project will be suitable for approval 
for a Special Use Permit. 
  
FINDINGS OF FACT: 
 
1. The item has been properly noticed and posted in compliance with Arizona Revised Statutes 
and Article 29.  
 
This item was posted in the White Mountain Independent, signs were posted on the property, and 
postcards were sent to all properties within 300’ of this property, in compliance with Arizona 
Revised Statutes and Article 29 of the Navajo County Zoning Ordinance. 
 
2. The proposal is in compliance with the Comprehensive Plan.

<!-- page 551 -->
This proposal complies with all relevant goals and policies of Navajo County’s Comprehensive 
Plan. 
 
3. The proposed use is compatible with the permissible uses in the zoning district in which the 
property is located. 
 
The proposed use should not have incompatibility issues with other permissible uses in the zoning 
district in which the property is located based on its location. 
 
4. The proposed use is compatible with the current and likely future uses of properties in the 
vicinity of the proposed use. 
 
There is already an existing cellular tower on the subject property, which has existed for 
approximately 7 years without issue.  This suggests there will be no issues over compatibility with 
current and likely future uses of properties in the vicinity of this proposal.  Additionally, with this 
tower being contained so internally on such a large parcel of land, there should not be issues imposed 
on neighboring properties.  
 
5. The proposed use will have minimal adverse effects on the public health, safety, and general 
welfare of properties in the vicinity of the proposed use. 
 
The proposed use should have minimal adverse effects on the public health, safety, and general 
welfare of properties in its vicinity.  
 
 
PUBLIC WORKS AND OTHER COUNTY DEPARTMENT’S COMMENTS: 
COUNTY ATTORNEY:  
No objection, as long as the applicant complies with all rules and regulations of Navajo County and 
the State of Arizona. 
Initial: B.S.C.  Date:   
      
ENGINEERING AND TRAFFIC: 
Engineering staff has reviewed the proposed Special Use Permit and has no Engineering objections. 
Initial: W.R.B. Date:  
   
DRAINAGE & FLOOD CONTROL: 
The Flood Control staff has reviewed the proposed Special Use Permit and has no objections. 
Initial: S.P. Date:  
   
PLANNING AND ZONING: 
The Planning and Zoning Staff has reviewed the proposed Special Use Permit and has no objections. 
Initial: S.P. Date:  
   
PLANNING AND ZONING RECOMMENDATION:  
 
The Navajo County Planning and Zoning Commission has recommended approval of the Special Use 
Permit on APN 212-20-147D, subject to the following conditions 
 
1. The Special Use Permit shall permit 1 (one) wireless communication tower, not to exceed

<!-- page 552 -->
105 feet in height, and accessory structures necessary for the operation of the tower on the 
subject property. 
 
2. The permitted Special Use shall be allowed to occur only  in the location shown on the 
approved site plan. 
 
3. The Special Use Permit shall run with the land. 
 
4. The applicant shall obtain a Building Permit from Navajo County for such tower prior to 
commencing construction. 
 
5. The applicant must meet all State and Fede ral requirements concerning communication 
towers prior to any building permits being issued. 
 
6. The tower shall be constructed with a monopine design, as indicated in the supplied plans.  
The owner of the tower shall maintain the pole, antennae, pine branches, and pine socks, as 
necessary. 
 
7. A seven (7) foot, solid fence, of material acceptable to the Planning and Zoning Director  
shall be constructed to enclose the leased property. 
 
8. If the permitted Special Use is not utilized within a twelve (12) month period  from the date 
of approval by the Board of Supervisors, this permit shall become null and void. 
 
9. Should the operator decide to abandon the use of the tower, they shall notify Navajo County 
Planning and Zoning that the tower is no longer being used. 
 
10. If the tower is no longer being utilized, the property owner shall cause, at their expense, the 
removal of all components of this tower within a 90-day period. 
 
11.  The owner shall provide a 105-foot, non-buildable easement from the tower base. 
 
12.  The owner shall provide reasonable cooperation to other public and private users who may 
be interested in collocation on this tower and shall consider such collocation upon 
commercially reasonable terms, unless such collocation is not technically feasible.

<!-- page 558 -->
Project Narrative for 
Verizon Wireless – AZ3 Kaywood 
3044 Lockwood Drive 
Lakeside, AZ 85929 
APN: 212-20-147D      
 
The proposed site is located on a  3.88-acre (169,012 sq-ft) parcel owned by Faith2Faith 
International. Verizon is  proposing to install a  new Wireless Communications Facility on the 
property tucked back in the property between existing trees in a manner that meets jurisdictional 
requirements for Navajo County in the City of Lakeside.  
 
In an effort to uphold the visual integrity  of the community and meet jurisdictional 
requirements, Verizon is proposing to install a 10 5’ Monopine (top of branches; top of steel is 
still 100’)  to help disguise the tower while also providing other carriers the opportunity to 
collocate on this same tower, thus reducing the number of towers in the community. Verizon will 
be installing six (6) antennas on the tower which will be mounted near the top of the tower and 
painted to match in an effort to disguise the antennas as well as installing “pine socks ” on the 
antennas and further improve on the visual and aesthetic character of the tower. The associated 
equipment would be enclosed within an equipment area below the tower that is being designed 
to blend in on the lot to disguise the equipment from publi c view. This location was chosen to 
camouflage the site as it will be placed amongst the existing pine trees toward the mid to back 
side of the property in an effort to both increase on the aesthetic character of the property while 
also providing additional coverage for the site/area.  
 
The purpose of proposed site  is in response to changes in technology to Verizon’s wireless 
services, and its commitment to providing better service throughout the area, to existing and 
potential customers.  There is a degradation in coverage and data capabilities in this area so this 
will help service the surrounding areas and fill in the current gaps in coverage. 
 
This parcel of property is Zoned R1 -10 with all surrounding properties zoned the same  (R1-
10). This property was chosen in an effort to increase the distance between the site and the 
surrounding neighbors while also still being in close enough in proximity to adequately service 
the area, to serve as capacity offload to the existing sectors at nearby sites ( which are currently 
overloaded and will be exhausted in the near future without the necessary offloading), as well as 
to increase E911 capabilities for the area. The WCF would be required to meet a setback distance 
from each lot line at least 1’ for each foot in additional height above the height limit from adjacent 
properties and cannot exceed 100’ in height, which this site meets. There were no power poles, 
buildings, or existing cell sites structurally capable (even with an extension) to collocate on, or 
that was tall enough or within close enough proximity to meet the RF expectations and needs for 
this location. Collocating on the existing Cell One  tower was explored; however, their site is not 
structurally capable of extending to the height that Verizon’s RF engineers need in order to both 
increase services in the area and offload the existing sites  that are currently overloaded .  This

<!-- page 559 -->
tower will be designed to be collocatable so that there will not be a need for another additional 
tower should another carrier wish to be in the area. 
 
In the previous submittal for this site, there were three neighbors, Mark McGhee, Deo 
Maynard, and Judy Kennedy, that showed or phoned into the zoning commission hearing that 
opposed the originally proposed monopole site. In an effort to understand their position and to 
find a solution that would work for everyone, we met with a representative of those surrounding 
neighbors that originally fought the monopole facility and their biggest gripe was that it was 
another “monopole” and not something more that would be less visually obtrusive. I asked them 
to put their thoughts into writing so that they could be included with this narrative and have been 
attached (please see below).  Since these emails were received, I  received letters of support as 
well (also attached).

<!-- page 560 -->
A public meeting was held on site on March 12th, 2020 at 5:00 PM for the originally proposed 
monopole and held for an hour and adjourned at 6:00 PM.  Notifications to the residents in the 
area were sent on March 2 nd for everyone  within 300’ of the property boundaries  per 
jurisdictional requirements for public outreach.  Community residents were notified of the 
scheduled public meeting so that any potential concerns could be addressed with them up front 
and before use permit submittal  with Navajo County.  One phone call was received  prior to the 
meeting in which the resident (Vicki Karkula) lives across Lockwood drive (but not directly across 
from the site) . This resident said she had no iss ue with the aesthetics of the site but was 
concerned about potential health issues.  She was told there are many studies that show that the 
site has less effect on people than the microwave in their kitchen and that there were many 
studies that she could r ead up on that show this site would meet or exceed any FCC or WHO 
(World Health Organization) requirements and she stated that there are also other studies that 
say the opposite.  She said she would be attending the meeting to learn more about the site and 
to ask more questions at that time. 
At the meeting, Vicki and her husband showed to the meeting and expressed their concerns 
regarding the potential health issues.  I told her I would pull additional resources that I could 
share with her to help mitigate her concerns.  She also stated that another neighbor was against 
it and would email Cody directly, but did not want to attend the meeting.  She said he had 
something that tested the area near their house, but I explained that most testing equipment 
available to the public has a certain frequency range in which it could test for and unless it was 
specific to each carriers frequencies, it would not be an accurate reading and that it could have 
been reading the power line usage at that time or a multitude of o ther things.  She said she did 
not realize that.  There were three others in attendance that were in support of the new tower. 
A list of the residents notified along with the letter that went out and a sign in sheet have 
been attached to this submittal for verification.  Overall, the meeting was a positive meeting, and 
everyone was pleasant.  Below is the map of the notification area as supplied from the county 
website. 
Those who originally shared their support are also happy with the proposed monopine design 
as they feel it will “blend in even better”.  The landowner is in support of the changes and has 
signed the attached application to show such support.  We have done everything possible to 
ensure we have neighborhood support for the proposed new monopine project as is evidenced 
by the attached and in this narrative and we are hopeful that the county will also support this 
project.  Below is a notification map from the original neighborhood notification.

<!-- page 561 -->
Citizen Participation and Notification Map of Properties Within 300’ 
 
 
It was mentioned that the staff may ask for a non- buildable easement for this site.  The 
landlord and Verizon a re asking that this stipulation not be applied to this site as it was not 
applied to the Cell one  tower on site that is not structurally capable of holding Verizon’s 
equipment with an extension at the height required. The non -buildable easement renders the 
area useless for the landlord; which he feels is an unreasonable request. The proposed tower is 
being built with the design to allow collocation and will be high enough that others WILL be able 
to use the site, if it works within their networks (meaning the location works for them and makes 
it possible to offload their other sites). 
 
The proposed site will meet the following standards: 
 
• All appropriate measures will be taken to conceal or disguise the tower and antennae 
from external view  
1. Reflected in the drawings on A-3, the proposed antennas and associated 
equipment installed on the tree will be painted to match the pine 
branches. 
a. “Pine needle socks” will also be used to further disguise the 
antennas.

<!-- page 562 -->
2. The steel tower will be encased with natural looking faux-bark cladding 
from the base of the pole to 5’ above the bottom of branches, the 
remainder of the pole is to be painted to match. 
3. Pine branches will start 15’ above grade/base of tower. There will be 3 
branches per linear foot. 
4. The equipment will be placed within an 8’ wooden fenced-in compound 
and will be constructed to blend in with existing building fences in look 
and color to blend in with its surroundings to the extent possible, to 
utilize materials, colors, textures, screening and landscaping that will 
blend them into the natural setting. There is no proposed additional 
landscaping due to the extent of the number of existing pine trees in the 
area that will help disguise the site from view. 
5. The Coaxial cables will be concealed within the Monopine 
6. Though there will be a plug for a back-up generator in case of emergency, 
there will not be one on site unless the site shuts down due to an 
emergency. 
7. There will be no climbing pegs installed on the tower. 
8. The tower will be built to accommodate at least one (1) additional carrier 
to help minimize the total number of towers throughout the community 
and it will be maintained in compliance with the standards contained in 
applicable state or local building codes/standards. 
9. The proposed equipment cabinets will be placed on a proposed 
equipment platform to protect them from any accumulated water in the 
area and to keep with the code requirements of not altering the natural 
grade/elevation of the site. 
10. There will not be any artificial lighting on the tower unless required by 
the FAA. 
11. The proposed ingress/egress will be from Lockwood Dr to the southeast 
of the site. 
 
• There will be no  interference from wireless communication transmitter, receptor, or 
other facility with police, fire, and emergency public safety communications. 
• The proposed wireless communication  tower and antennas shall be in compliance with 
all Federal Communications Commission (FCC) regulations, including those protecting the 
public health and those protecting historic districts. 
• The proposed tower/antennas will be in compliance with all standards established by the 
Federal Environmental Protection Agency and Federal Communications Commission 
(FCC) regarding potential health and safety hazards.  The submittal of information, and 
the review of the application by the Department of Operations, shall be in accordance 
with all applicable standards.

<!-- page 563 -->
• The Verizon Wireless site is equipped with the E-911emergency response system in time 
of need. 
Also attached to this narrative will be: 
• Plans showing site plan and  elevation drawings of the exterior of each element of the 
proposed wireless communication facility. 
• RF Justification inclusive of  propagation maps showing the need for the height and site 
location. 
• Photo Simulations of the site. 
• Vicinity Maps with legal descriptions (included on the survey but submitted separate from 
the plans and also included in the site plans). 
• Recorded Property Deed and Plat Map. 
• Resolution 18-13 with stipulations for the existing tower on site. 
*Verizon Certifies that the wireless communication facility, as represented in this application, will 
comply with all FAA, FCC and other applicable regulations.

<!-- page 583 -->
Project Need Overview:
Confidential and proprietary materials for authorized Verizon personnel and outside agencies only. Use, disclosure or distribution of this material is not permitted to any unauthorized persons or third parties except by  
written agreement.
The primary objective for this project is to improve service quality along W. White Mountain Blvd, Lockwood 
Dr. and the developing commercial and residentialareas in the area. The increase in the number of users in 
the area and those who travel through are creating a degradation theeffective propagation of a signal with 
the higher frequency technologies, so antenna height becomes a significant requirement. This site also 
provides capacity offload from Surroundingsites within the community,which are significantly overloaded. 
Detail is provided supporting these issues on slides 8-10.
Our engineering data shows that this area is experiencing 4G data overloads (See page 9). The area between 
the surrounding sites to the south, north, northwest and southeast of the proposed site,with its coverage area 
shown in red on pages 5 & 6 shows the need to have some of the area it covers moved onto another site to 
allow it to keep performing well. The proposed site will move commercial traffic onto a more localized site, 
better able to serve this area as well as allowing future growth into 5G.
Additional details and explanations follow in thispresentation.

<!-- page 584 -->
Introduction:
Confidential and proprietary materials for authorized Verizon personnel and outside agencies only. Use, disclosure or distribution of this material is not permitted to any unauthorized persons or third parties except by  
written agreement.
Coverage and/or capacity deficiencies are the two main drivers that prompt the need for a  new wireless 
communications facility (WCF). Most WCF provide a mixture of both capacity  and coverage for the 
benefit of the end user.
Coverage describes the existence or lack of wireless service in an area. The request for  improved service 
often comes from our customers or emergency services personnel that  have no service or poor service. 
Coverage used to refer to the ability to make or place a call  in vehicles, however, as usage patterns have 
shifted, coverage is now determined based on  whether or not sufficient WCF exist to provide a reliable 
signal inside of buildings and  residential areas, as well. Historically, when wireless was still in its infancy, 
coverage was  the primary means to measure the effectiveness of the network in a given area.
Capacity is the metric used to determine if sufficient wireless resources exist and is now the  primary 
means to measure how a community’s wireless needs are being addressed. “Five  bars” no longer means 
guaranteed coverage and capacity because each WCF has a limited  amount of resources to handle 
voice calls, data connections and data volume. When these  limits are reached and the WCF becomes 
overloaded (meaning there is more demand than  signal to service it), the user experience quickly 
degrades preventing customers from  making/receiving calls or getting applications to run. A WCF short 
on capacity could also  make internet connections time out or delay information to emergency response
personnel.

<!-- page 585 -->
Coverage is best shown via coverage maps. RF engineers use tools that take into  account terrain, 
vegetation, building types, and WCF specifics to model the existing  coverage and prediction what we expect 
to see with the addition of a proposed WCF.
Coverage also changes depending on which frequencies are used. Most phones  today use 3G at 800 MHZ 
or 4G at 700 MHz spectrum which are considered low  frequencies. Low frequencies can travel further 
distances than then the higher 1900  MHz and 2100 MHz frequencies now being employed due to increased 
capacity  demands. Operating at higher frequencies makes it necessary for carriers to install  substantially 
more wireless facilities to achieve the same coverage as one tower  operating on the lowerfrequencies.
Confidential and proprietary materials for authorized Verizon personnel and outside agencies only. Use, disclosure or distribution of this material is not permitted to any unauthorized persons or third parties except by  
written agreement.

Current Coverage without Kaywood 
Site

<!-- page 588 -->
Capacity is the amount of resources that a WCF has to service customer demand.  Verizon utilizes 
sophisticated programs and customer feedback to monitor current  usage trends and to forecast future 
needs. Because it takes an average of 2-3  years to complete a WCF, we have to start the process of 
adding a new WCF  several years in advance of when the WCF will beneeded.
Location, Location,Location. A good capacity WCF needs to be in the center of  a user population which 
ensures that traffic is evenly distributed around the WCF. A  typical WCF is configured into three sectors 
(like a pie cut into three pieces), with  each slice (sector) having 33% of the WCF resources. If one sector is
under-utilized, it’s resources can not necessarily be diverted to another sector.  Therefore, optimal 
performance is only obtained when all three sectors have an  even trafficdistribution.

written agreement.
Explanation of WirelessCapacity

<!-- page 589 -->
Wireless Data Growth
Each year Verizon sees large increases in how much data its customers need. As the resolution of the  
pictures we send increases, the quality of the video we watch improves and the complexity of the  
applications grow, we commonly see tremendous growth year-over-year. 
Machine to Machine communications will also increase the data burden on wireless networks, as over the  
next five (5) years more and more services that improve our safety and make our lives easier will be  
available over the wireless infrastructure , such as:
- Cars that notify 911 when an airbagdeploys.
- “Driverless” cars needing traffic data and maps to reach your destination as quickly aspossible.
- Medical monitors that will alert us should a loved one neglect taking their prescriptiondrugs.
- Home alarms that notify you when your child arrives home from school.
- Smart street lights that notify the city when they are not working.
- City garbage cans that let people know when they need to beemptied.
- Tracking watches will aid in finding lost Alzheimerpatients.

Explanation of Wireless DataGrowth

<!-- page 590 -->
Summary: The existing communications sites cannot support the data traffic in the large area they cover and are already becoming
overloaded  as shown above by the blue use line rising above the red exhaustion threshold. When this occurs 4G data speeds slow to 
unacceptable  levels.
Detail below:
The graph above shows FDV (Forward Data Volume) which is a measurement of the customer data usage that this sector currently serves.  
The blue line shows the daily data use on this sector of the wireless facility site. The yellow line is a projection based on the last years  
usage to show when we expect to see our customers begin to see their data speeds begin to slow down. The red line is the limit where the  
sector becomes exhausted and service starts to degrade. The point in time where we see the yellow line go above the red line is when we  
will start seeing service begin to degrade. Service will quickly degrade after that point as usage continues to grow.
To aid in resolving this, we ask to add a 3-sector communications facility as proposed to improve wireless service capacity and coverage in  
this area by offloading commercial traffic from overloaded sector s with the proposed site.

<!-- page 591 -->
A macro is necessary in this environment due to its unique characteristics such as  tower height, ability to 
deploy additional technologies, and large coverage footprint.  Combined these features make a macro 
solution ideal for maximizing coverage and  capacity in the network. The proposed coverage area includes 
the housing developments as well as the commercial areas surrounding this site. Without a macro solution 
in  place in the area the additional user traffic generated by these developments will place a greater capacity 
strain on the existing WCFs and further degrade performance.
In contrast, small cells are characterized by low tower height and minimal  deployable technologies which 
would not be able to accomplish the areaobjective.

written agreement.

Board of Supervisors Regular 9. a. 1.        
Meeting Date: 01/12/2021  
Title: Bank Account for Revenues (deposits/credit/online)
Submitted By: Melissa Buckley, Clerk of the Board of Supervisors
Department: Board of Supervisors
Presented By: Mark Honberger
Motion before the Board:
Consideration and possible approval of White Mountain Lakes County Recreation Improvement
District to open up a separate bank account for the purpose of collecting revenues. 
Background:
THE SITUATION:  The WMLCRID would like to solve a number of issues relating to its ability
to collect revenue, by improving the financial processes involved.
  
CREDIT CARD PAYMENTS: Currently, WMLCRID can only accept cash or check
payments. The District would like to be able to also take credit card payments at the lake, as
this will allow us to better serve our citizens as well as allow us to move into online
payments. Unfortunately, the current financial accounting processes do not allow for this to
take place. At this time, all revenue collection for WMLCRID is processed through the
county financial accounts, which will not allow WMCLCRID to set up credit card
payments due to its status as a separate Special District.
1.
  
REVENUE DEPOSITS: Currently, in order for WMLCRID make a deposit of
checks, staff delivers them to the county offices, where they are sent via the
interoffice currier up to the county offices in Holbrook. Unfortunately, the District
cannot deposit cash via the currier. The only way for cash to be deposited is for Lake
Staff to drive to Holbrook regularly, or for County Staff to drive to White Mountain
Lakes regularly. This has proven quite inefficient for both parties involved.
2.
Recommendation: 
A POTENTIAL SOLUTION:  WMLCRID Lake staff has worked with County Staff and the
County Finance Director to determine a potential solution for the situation described above.
  
A SEPARATE WMLCRID BANK ACCOUNT:  Staff is proposing that WMLCRID be
allowed to set up a separate bank account for the purpose of collecting revenues.  Funds
collected this account would be then be transferred at regular intervals (typically monthly)
to the County financial systems. The account would be located at a local bank in either
1.

<!-- page 602 -->
Show Low, Snowflake, or Taylor, and County Staff would have access monitor or audit the
account as often as needed. The benefits of this solutions are as follows:
  
CREDIT CARD PAYMENTS: A separate WMLCRID bank account will allow the
district to begin collecting credit card payments, and eventually online payments as well.
a.
  
EFFICIENT DEPOSITS FOR CASH AND CHECKS: A separate WMLCRID bank
account will allow the district to make deposits as often as needed, multiple times
each week, at a convenient location. This solution will make the District more
efficient.
b.
  
BETTER CUSTOMER SERVICE: The residents that frequent the lake have
expressed interest in being able to pay via credit card, and will truly appreciate the
convenience of being able to pay online.
c.
